NAVAJO COUNTY BOARD OF SUPERVISORS 
Fern Benally • Alberto L. Peshlakai • Jason E. Whiting • Daryl Seymore • Dawnafe Whitesinger
“We are Navajo County”
 
NOTICE OF PUBLIC HEARING AND AGENDA
Tuesday, March 12, 2024
NAVAJO COUNTY GOVERNMENTAL COMPLEX
BOARD OF SUPERVISORS' CHAMBERS
100 EAST CODE TALKERS DRIVE
HOLBROOK, AZ 86025
ALL SUPERVISORS WILL BE ATTENDING THIS MEETING VIRTUALLY
 
Pursuant to A.R.S. §38-431.02(H), the public body will have physical access to the meeting place one (1) hour prior to the start of
the meeting. The Board may vote to meet in a closed executive session to discuss certain matters and for legal advice on any item
as indicated on the following agenda, pursuant to A.R.S. § 38-431.03(A)(3). Items on the agenda may be considered out of order at
the Chairperson's discretion. A copy of the agenda background material provided to the Board, except items to be considered in the
executive session, is available for public inspection at the Navajo County website, www.navajocountyaz.gov 
WATCH THE MEETING LIVE AT: www.navajocountyaz.gov/660
All public comments will need to be made in person or in writing. Written comments will be received by the Clerk of the Board's
office twenty-four (24) hours prior to the Board meeting, at melissa.buckley@navajocountyaz.gov. If further accommodation is
needed, please contact the Clerk of the Board's office, at (928) 524-4053 twenty-four (24) hours prior to the Board Meeting, so
accommodation can be arranged.  
Persons with disabilities who need accommodation to attend or participate in the meeting may contact the Clerk of the Board’s
office at least forty-eight (48) hours prior to the meeting, so accommodation can be arranged.
9:00 a.m. Call To Order: Invocation and Pledge of Allegiance

3 minutes. At the close of the call to the public, Board members may not respond to any
comments but may respond to criticism, ask staff to review a matter or ask that a matter be
placed on a future agenda.

LIBRARY DISTRICT: Board of Directors Session: 
 
REGULAR AGENDA: 
1. Presentation and recognition of the following Community Libraries:
Pinedale Public Library, Clay Springs Public Library, Rim Community
Library, and Woodruff Community Library: 
Jennifer Volkert shared her appreciation for being able to recognize the
volunteer libraries.  She stated that these are the only libraries run
completely by volunteers in the State of Arizona, and that they gave 6,587
hours of their time volunteering in the libraries.  She indicated that
Pinedale received the Arizona Volunteer or the Year award last year and
she thanked them for their time and commitment.  She stated that there
are no paid positions, that there is no budget for books, materials, or
programs, and that all the work done is from the kindness of their hearts. 
She recognized each of the representatives from the libraries that were
present and presented them with a check to support their libraries.
Linda Hill, Pinedale Public Library volunteer, thanked the Board for the
support. She stated that they do have a couple of fundraisers but that
without the support checks they would not be able to continue.
Era Harris, Rim Community Library volunteer, shared her appreciation to
the Board for the support.  She indicated that they do several fundraisers
to help, but that the support checks help them a lot.
Ms. Volkert thanked the Board for supporting the libraries. 
Supervisor Seymore stated it’s a privilege to be able to work with the
libraries and talked about how it’s a great resource for the communities. 
He indicated that reading, learning, and knowledge is everything.  He
thanked them for volunteering, indicating that it wouldn’t work without the
volunteers.
Vice-Chairwoman Benally thanked them for all of their hours
volunteering in the libraries.  She talked about her love for the library and
the number of books that are available.  She further talked about the new
  
                                                                    3 of 19 
DRAFT

<!-- page 7 -->
library being built in Winslow, indicating that she is excited about it.
Chairman Whiting expressed his appreciation to the volunteers for their
time and dedication, indicating that they deserve a lot of credit for their
willingness to serve the members of the communities and those who use
the libraries.      
Vice-Chairwoman Benally made a motion to return to regular session,
motion seconded by Supervisor Seymore.  Vote unanimous approving
the motion. 
 
NORTHEASTERN ARIZONA INNOVATIVE WORKFORCE SOLUTIONS: 
 
a. Presentation to Lisa Grannis: 
Jeremy Flowers recognized and thanked Lisa Grannis for her
extraordinary work in the WIOA program over the past two years,
indicating that the program couldn’t have functioned without her.  He
talked about how she helped restore staff morale, made sure that their
voices were heard and indicated that she has always been supportive of
the team and helped through all of the challenging times.  He stated that
she has a great attitude and a sunny disposition. He thanked her for all
that she has done for the program.  He presented her with a gift of
appreciation for her service. 
Lisa Grannis stated that she has great expectations for the program, that
they have a great team, and they are headed in the right direction. She
stated that the team at Navajo County has been great in helping them. 
Bryan Layton thanked Ms. Grannis for all of the support she has given to
the program.  He talked about her contagious attitude.  He further talked
about her husband Frank, who was previously an employee of Navajo
County.  He thanked them both for their service to the county.
Chairman Whiting presented Ms. Grannis with a gift of appreciation for
her service. 
 
  
ADMINISTRATIVE SERVICES:
 
a. Consideration of award of Contract No. B23-09-021 for the Navajo County
Complex Remodel to Pointe Companies, Inc and to authorize change
order authority to the County Manager for 10% of the contract amount,
and authorize the County Manager to execute the necessary documents : 
A presentation was shown.  Grant Evans stated this is an ARPA project
that they have been working on for a while and that it’s exciting to be at
the point to move into construction.  He talked about the areas in the
complex where the improvements will be made. Including the restrooms,
kitchen, and employee breakroom.  He indicated that there were three
challenges identified that they wanted to address, including difficult
customer access to services, the current lack of entry restrictions, the
lack of training space and the aging of restroom facilities.  He talked about
the objectives of the project, including simplify and improve customer
access to the Recorder/Voter Registration, the Assessor, the Treasurer,
and the Board of Supervisors/Clerk of the Board; improve safety and
security by strategically locating new badge access doors and access
  
                                                                    4 of 19 
DRAFT

<!-- page 8 -->
points to restrict access; create a more functional training space and
remodel/update restrooms to improve functionality and include ADA
compliance.  He reviewed the floor plan, addressing the areas where
changes will be made for customer access, the badge access points that
will be added, the training space, the breakroom, and the remodeled
restrooms.  He talked about the budget for the project, indicating that
$150,000 are from the APRA allocation, $150,000 General Funds and
$211,500 with come from the Local Assistance Consistency Fund.  He
reviewed the projected schedule, stating that this is a six-month contract. 
He talked about aging and deteriorating sewer pipes and indicated that
they will be doing a separate contract for a cured-in-place pipe lining,
which will allow them to not have to cut up the floor and will be done at the
same time.  He talked about the post construction item that will need to be
completed but are not be in this contract, including securing counters,
refresh the training space, breakroom, and kitchen area, indicating that it
will mostly likely be done inhouse.  He stated that they received one bid
for this project, that it is a local contractor, and that staff is recommending
approval of the item.  
Chairman Whiting thanked staff for working on this project and for
making sure this aligns with the long-term needs of the building. 
Supervisor Whitesinger talked about the impact with COVID and
wanting to provide better services to the public.  She stated that she is
grateful for the work and investment so that those services will continue
and that the organization can continue to provide the best support to the
community members.   
Supervisor Whitesinger made a motion to award of Contract No.
B23-09-021 for the Navajo County Complex Remodel to Pointe
Companies, Inc and to authorize change order authority to the County
Manager for 10% of the contract amount and authorize the County
Manager to execute the necessary documents, motion seconded
by Supervisor Peshlakai.  Vote unanimous approving the motion.

b. Consideration and appointment of two members to fill the vacant seats on
                                                                    5 of 19 
DRAFT

<!-- page 9 -->
b. Consideration and appointment of two members to fill the vacant seats on
the Silver Creek Flood Control District Board of Directors: 
Rayleen Richards stated the Silver Creek Flood Control District was
supposed to conduct an election in October 2023, that they were given a
quote from her office and that they decide to do it on their own.  She
further stated that the election was not held, which left two vacancies and
that they contacted her office in December to let them know the election
was not held and see what the next steps were.  She reviewed the ARS
48-2681(c), indicating that if a vacancy occurs it shall be filled by
appointment by the Board of Supervisors until the next regular election for
the district.  She indicated that they have received three letters of interest,
William Baldwin, Brad Hatch, and Gus Lundberg.  She stated that staff
recommends that two members be appointed.  She indicated that the
Silver Creek Flood Control District will need to conduct an election in
October 2025. 
Chairman Whiting disclosed that he tried to reach out to all three
applicants to understand their interests.  He stated that he talked with Gus
Lundberg and William Baldwin and that he left a message for Brad Hatch. 
He reviewed the discussion that he had with the individuals and talked
about the value each of them would have on the board with the various
backgrounds. 
Supervisor Seymore made a motion to appoint of two members, Brad
Hatch and Gus Lundberg, to fill the vacant seats on the Silver Creek
Flood Control District Board of Directors, motion seconded by Supervisor
Whitesinger.  Vote unanimous approving the motion. 
 
  
FACILITIES MANAGEMENT: 
 
a. Consideration of an agreement with the APS Solar Communities Program
to install covered solar parking structures in the Employee Parking lot at
the Navajo County Governmental Complex in Holbrook : 
Bret Starns stated that the agreement is for the APS Solar Communities
Project, and that it would be covered parking in the employee parking lot. 
He indicated that they applied approximately a year ago, that they were
accepted, that they provided a vendor to look at and design the solar
parking cover.  He indicated that it’s a good partnership with APS and
stated that staff recommends approval.
Chairman Whiting inquired if there is a fiscal cost to the county.
Mr. Starns indicated that there is no fiscal cost, that it’s a twenty-year
contract and at the end of the twenty years it can be renegotiated to
continue the project, or they will come back at their cost and remove it. 
Chairman Whiting inquired where the covered parking would be.
Mr. Starns showed a diagram of where the covered parking would be. 
Supervisor Whitesinger made a motion to approve an agreement with
the APS Solar Communities Program to install covered solar parking
structures in the Employee Parking lot at the Navajo County
Governmental Complex in Holbrook, motion seconded
by Vice-Chairwoman Benally.  Vote unanimous approving the motion.

1. Presentation of 2023 Public Health Employee of the Year, Bencita
Bowman:
Janelle Linn stated that in Public Health they have an Employee of the
Month and at the end of the year they review the nominations and select
their Public Health Employee of the Year.  She indicated that Bencita
Bowman was selected as the 2023 Employee of the Year.  She stated
that Ms. Bowman has done many things that have helped the department
to better serve the public and indicated that she is devoted.  She talked
about how Ms. Bowman mentors the staff and prepared them for audit
preparation, how she has reduced their audit findings and increased their
financial accuracy and quality and help them develop and improve
financial policies and procedures and provided training and education. 
She thanked Ms. Bowman and presented her with a gift of appreciation.
Supervisor Seymore stated that it takes employees like Ms. Bowman to
make things work by going above and beyond. He indicated that they
appreciate her dedication.  
Ms. Bowman talked about the different departments she has worked in
over the past twenty-eight years.  She shared her appreciation to those
she works with and for the recognition that they have given her, indicating
that it means a lot.

2. Amy Stradling stated that one of the most impacted populations for the
fatality review was children and that they are trying to focus on making a
difference in the lives of the children and families of Navajo County
ensuring it’s safer for them.  She indicated that over nine percent of
children deaths in Arizona in 2022 were due to motor vehicle crashes. 
She stated that they now have staff that can instruct so they can train new
certified technicians.  She further stated that they have certified
technicians that offer car seat safety classes, which is free so that the
communities can get education of the importance of car seats, and how to
properly install their child restraints to make sure it’s appropriate for their
child.  She indicated that car seats can reduce injury by seventy-one
percent and that forty-nine percent of child restraints are misused.  She
stated that they have limited grant funding so she started researching
other avenues for funding that could address the safety issues in our
communities and found this grant that they were awarded $10,816 that
could purchase new child restraints.  She further stated that after
individuals complete the course and learn how to properly install the
seats, they would be provided with a new seat free of charge.  She stated
that staff is recommending approval.
Supervisor Seymore talked about the need for car seats and for
installing them correctly.  He addressed the need to help people be safer
and indicated that he is grateful to the Health Department for their work on
this.
Chairman Whiting stated that when the Department sees a need, they fill
the need and look for opportunities to find additional funding to help our
citizens.  He thanked the Health Department for their work on this.  
Supervisor Seymore made a motion to approve the grant agreement
between the Arizona Governor's Office of Highway Safety and Navajo
County Public Health Services District to purchase new child restraints to
issue to qualifying residents, motion seconded by Supervisor
Whitesinger.  Vote unanimous approving the motion. 
Supervisor Whitesinger made a motion to return to regular session,
motion seconded by Supervisor Seymore.  Vote unanimous approving
the motion.

a. Recognition of new County Engineer, William Flake: 
Madhav Mundle announced that Public Works has promoted Will Flake to
be the new County Engineer.  He talked about his experience and
education.  He stated that he is a great team player and has excellent
judgment.  He thanked the Board and the County Manager for their
support of this promotion. 
Will Flake stated that he appreciates this opportunity, indicating that he
was born and raised in Navajo County, that it has been a great
opportunity to raise family in Navajo County, and that it is an honor and

Supervisor Seymore talked about working with Mr. Flake, indicating that

b. Consideration of an Intergovernmental Agreement with the State of
Arizona, Department of Transportation for Concho Highway, Golden
Horse Ln - White Antelope Rd Pavement Rehabilitation:
Madhav Mundle stated that the HURF Exchange Program was created by
the State Legislature in 1997, that it had been put on hold and that the
program is now back.  He indicated that this program authorizes ADOT to
exchange state highway funds for federal surface transportation funds,
which allows local governments to utilize HURF funds and build projects
faster without out needing to comply with time consuming and expensive
federal requirements.  He stated that the program is offered at ADOT’s
discretion, that Navajo County is eligible for it in 2024, so Public Works
applied for it.  He stated that the funds will be applied to a District Three
project for payment preservation on Concho Highway.  He talked about
the location of the project, indicating that it will be approximately three
miles, and that they will get a little over seven-hundred-fifty-five thousand
dollars as part of the HURF Exchange and that they will also be using
some of the Navajo County HURF funds to make the project happen.  He
stated that staff recommends approval.
Supervisor Seymore made a motion to approve an Intergovernmental
Agreement with the State of Arizona, Department of Transportation for
Concho Highway, Golden Horse Ln - White Antelope Rd Pavement
Rehabilitation, motion seconded by Supervisor Peshlakai.  Vote
unanimous approving the motion. 
 
  
PLANNING & DEVELOPMENT: 
 
1. PUBLIC HEARING: Consideration and possible adoption of Resolution
Number 10-2024, approving/denying text amendment to Article 22,
Sections 2202, 2203, and 2207, regarding recreational vehicle placement: 
A presentation was shown. Cody Cooper stated that this is a proposed
amendment to the Recreational Vehicle Placement section of the Zoning
Ordinance.  He indicated that this is not an amendment to the actual
placement but to clarify short term occupancy and storage versus general
placement, due to reports from Code Enforcement regarding complaints. 
  
                                                                    9 of 19 
DRAFT

<!-- page 13 -->
placement, due to reports from Code Enforcement regarding complaints. 
He reviewed the proposed changes and stated that this will hopefully help
for those that have multi-family properties from being reported for storing
their RV’s.  He stated that staff, as well as the Planning and Zoning
Commission, recommend approval. He indicated that staff have not
received any comments from the public.
Chairman Whiting indicated that this is not changing anything, that it’s
just clarifying.
Mr. Cooper stated that it’s just clarify the language by adding definitions
and clarifying which zones are appropriate. 
Supervisor Seymore inquired if this is storage or living in an RV.
Mr. Cooper stated that the short-term occupancy is staying in an RV,
which is once per calendar year for a period not to exceed thirty-days,
which is already in the ordinance, but that they are clarifying that to say
within the multifamily you can’t do short term occupancy, but you can do
the storage.  He stated that it’s a little of both, but just clarifying which one
is allowed in what area. 
A discussion was held regarding the different situations that would be
acceptable for the proposed ordinance. 
Supervisor Whitesinger talked about how developing appropriate
language to help define specific usage is really important.  She thanked
the Department for taking the steps to specifically state some of the
clarifications. 
Mr. Cooper stated that they appreciate the support the Board has had
with code compliance so they’re able to work with staff to help refine the
ordinances to make it easier for the public to understand.   
 
Chairman Whiting opened the meeting for public hearing.  There being
no comments, the public hearing was closed. 
Supervisor Seymore made a motion to adopt Resolution Number
10-2024, approving text amendment to Article 22, Sections 2202, 2203,
and 2207, regarding recreational vehicle placement, motion seconded
by Supervisor Whitesinger.  Vote unanimous approving the motion. 
 
2. PUBLIC HEARING: Consideration and possible adoption of Resolution
Number 11-2024, approving/denying text amendment to Article 29,
Section 2907, regarding citizen review: 
A presentation was shown. Cody Cooper stated that this section is on
citizen review.  He talked about the history, indicating that the statutes
require that when there is a zone change that a citizen review process
occur, that Navajo County does require applicants to mail out or host a
meeting to notify the public ahead of a hearing but that the statute is
limited to a zone change.  He indicated that there are a number of items
that come through that are not zone changes, such as special use
permits or plats, that the public want to be informed and heard on these
items.  He indicated they are recommending to strike the existing citizen
review and put in a process that would apply to all items that go to the
Planning and Zoning Commission and then come before the Board.  He
indicated that this would allow full openness and disclosure to the
community regarding the projects prior to the Commission and Board
  
                                                                    10 of 19 
DRAFT

<!-- page 14 -->
meetings to see if the applicant could work with the public or staff to
resolve some of the concerns prior to the meetings.   He stated that this
would require a public meeting for the applicant to listen to the concerns
of the citizens and talked about the requirements for the meetings.  He
stated that staff works to take care of the concerns for public health and
safety, but they want to expand upon that is possible with this process. 
He addressed instances where the applicant could apply for a waiver.  He
stated that staff recommends approval of the item and that the Planning &
Zoning Commission unanimously recommend approval.
Supervisor Seymore inquired if they would be responsible for extra costs
for the extra notification.
Mr. Cooper stated that the property owner would be responsible for the
mailings, but that staff would be able to help with generating a list.
Supervisor Seymore indicated that he feels this is a good process by
trying to address some of the potential issues and that it’s proactive. 
 
Chairman Whiting opened the meeting for public hearing.  There being
no comments, the public hearing was closed. 
Chairman Whiting inquired if this would increase the length of the
process.
Mr. Cooper indicated that not necessarily and talked about the meetings
that they already have.  He indicated that they would advise the applicant
of the process at first approach.  He stated that it can be worked into the
current timelines without extending the process. 
Supervisor Whitesinger made a motion to adopt Resolution Number
11-2024, approving text amendment to Article 29, Section 2907,
regarding citizen review, motion seconded by Supervisor Seymore. 
Vote unanimous approving the motion. 
 
3. PUBLIC HEARING: Consideration and possible adoption of Resolution
Number 12-2024, approving/denying text amendment Subdivision
Regulations and Requirements, Sections 8.1 and 8.3, regarding street
design and sanitary sewage disposal : 
A presentation was shown. Cody Cooper stated that they have been
analyzing the subdivision requirements to make sure they are on par with
other jurisdictions in the state and that they are working with developers
and the public to make sure that subdivisions are moving forward in the
public interest for health, safety, and welfare. He talked about the
proposed changes and the current policies for paved roads, sewer, and
septic.  He noted that anything within the subdivision ordinance there can
be an application for a waiver to the terms if it’s found to be impracticable
or if it doesn’t provide any provisions for health and safety.  He stated that
staff, as well as the Planning and Zoning Commission, recommend
approval of the item.
Supervisor Seymore inquired if there are any current projects that this
could affect.
Mr. Cooper stated that there are none currently in process that would be
altered.

4. PUBLIC HEARING: Consideration and possible adoption of Resolution
Number 13-2024 for a Special Use Permit to allow for the construction
and operation of a 1,100 MW solar energy generation facility, 1,100 MW
Battery Energy Storage System, and associated facilities on
approximately 7,485 acres located northwest of the Snowflake area : 
A presentation was shown. Tyler Richards stated that the battery energy
storage system and associated facilities would be located approximately
seventeen miles northwest of Snowflake and would be five parcels in total
and that it is owned by Rocking Chair Ranch, Inc.  He talked about the
zoning for the location and the project components, which will include
solar modules and their racking system, cabling to convert the panel
outputs, a battery energy storage system, permanent unpaved roads
within the facility, perimeter fencing, and an operations and management
building.  He addressed the site plan with the layout of the facility.  For the
staff analysis, he stated that this project does meet a number of goals
within the comprehensive plan.  He stated that the applicant has worked
to identify cultural resources, that they have a plan in they do encounter
any and that they will use best practices to preserve any cultural or
historical sites on the project.  He indicated that they have completed
surveys to assess the wildlife and habitat resources within the area and
that there should be minimal impact on wildlife and protected species
within the project area.  He talked about the multiple points of access to
the project.  He stated that staff, as well as the Planning & Zoning
Commission, recommend approval subject to the conditions in the staff
report.
Supervisor Whitesinger inquired as to the special conditions; how does
it go outside of the ordinance and the impact on the land.   
Tyler indicated that the conditions are things that the applicant has to do
in order for the project to move forward, such as complying with all state,
federal, and local laws and those required by the jurisdiction, to ensure
that the applicant is fully complying with all of the regulations.     
Applicant, Samantha Chody, with Enfinity Global, thanked the Board for
consideration of the project and stated that they hope to create a
partnership and have it be successful for everyone.
Supervisor Seymore inquired as to the timeframe it this were approved.
Dale Harris, with Enfinity Global, stated that they are looking at starting
construction around September 26, 2024, but that it’s subject to change
due to it being a big project with a lot of moving parts.   
 
Chairman Whiting opened the meeting for public hearing.  There being
no comments, the public hearing was closed.

COUNTY BUSINESS UPDATE: Report from County Manager, County Attorney
and Board members : 
Supervisor Seymore thanked the Navajo Nation for sportsmanship in the
basketball semi-finals and talked about the support of the community for the teams. 
He stated that it was great to see the students from the many different schools
participating in the basketball championships.  He shared his appreciation to staff
for all that they do behind the scenes and to the elected officials as they work
towards making a year that we can be grateful for moving into budget session.
Supervisor Peshlakai reported on the meetings he has been attending and
thanked staff for coordinating the meetings.  He stated that they meet with the
Director of the Navajo Division of Transportation and the Director for the Hopi
Department of Transportation to address tribal transportation concerns.  He
indicated that he continues to meet with the ten chapters within his district and
attend the different functions.  He talked about meetings regarding the Winslow
Levee, including the visit by Colonel Baker and going before the committee in
Flagstaff. He thanked staff and those that have coordinated with the different
stakeholders. 
Supervisor Whitesinger extended her appreciation to the Superintendent of
Schools on the spelling bee and congratulated all of those involved and stated that
there is a science fair coming up.  She talked about the Community Research
Leadership Board for the American Indian Structural Health Hear Disease
Partnership that she sits on, indicating that they have several meetings this past
                                                                    13 of 19

week and that they are trying to create a space to educate individuals on health,
and encourage them to do the screenings.  She indicated that Native Americans
have a higher rate of heart disease and that they need to educate community
members.  She talked about the Native Woman Scholars, that she is a Board
Member of, indicating that they work on fund raising to provide scholarships for
young women who attend college through the nation and that they will be
fundraising projects in May that she will share information for soon.  She stated that
they have many of the students that they have provided scholarships for that have
graduated and are now working in the community.  She thanked Public Health for
the work groups that are being developed for particular initiatives to support the top
priorities.  She stated that she has been able to sit in a couple of the meetings,
including the Mental Health Work Group, and indicated that she is grateful for the
individuals who are committed to the organization and have the knowledge base to
support our communities.  She advised that she has a Town Hall in Pinetop on
Wednesday, February 28, 2024, at 6:00 p.m. for anyone who would like to attend.  
Vice-Chairwoman Benally congratulated the high school students who have

is working with NDOT to get some road maintenance done in Pinon and talked

Chairman Whiting talked about the NRWG meeting, indicating that when the

that Chris Pasterz has been a great hire and is actively working with the different
communities.

BUDGET WORK SESSION: Pursuant to ARS § 38-431.02 and ARS § 38-431(3),
notice is hereby given to the members of the Navajo County Board of Supervisors
and to the Public that a quorum of the Board of Supervisors may be present for a
work session immediately following the Board of Supervisors Agenda in the Board
Chambers where no official action will be taken : 
Bryan Layton thanked the Board for their time today. He stated that next week staff
will start budget meetings with Department Directors and Elected Officials and want
  
                                                                    14 of 19 
DRAFT

<!-- page 18 -->
to make sure we are working toward the same goals.
Jayson Vowell started the presentation by addressing the overall budget
philosophy indicating that we need a balanced budget required by law, we need to
have a structural balance which is making sure our recurring revenues meet or
exceed our recurring expenses, and that we grow and maintain adequate reserves
by having a cautious approach to growing programs and identifying one time
revenues fund one time projects.
Mr. Vowell reviewed the General Fund balance. He spoke about the General Fund
balance in 2014 vs today. He indicated that we finally got healthy about 2021. He
spoke about carryover cash from projects that did not get completed.
Mr. Vowell went on to talk about the major budget impacts and accomplishments
that have been made. He discussed the approval of the Jail District sales tax in
January 2021 and also the implementation of the CARES Act funding and ARPA
funding which brought in $8 million for Public Health and Public Safety in fiscal year
21 and 22. He stated that the 2020 jump was due to COVID relief. Mr. Vowell
explained the growth in sales tax, indicating that people had money in their pockets
from the stimulus. He stated that we have seen the growth in sales tax and
continue to be responsible for those dollars. He further stated that we have
continued to reduce and stabilize the property tax rate. Mr. Vowell spoke about the
refinancing of the PSPRS Pension and indicated a pension contingency reserve
fund of $2 million. Mr. Vowell discussed flexibility transfers from the library district
and flood control district and how we were able to eliminate those. He also
discussed 100% ALTCS and ACHHHS have been returned to the general fund. He
stated that we used to take 50% from the general fund for those programs.
Mr. Vowell spoke about the employee benefit reserve funds. He indicated that we
were able to take a million and a half from the reserve fund. He further indicated
that we have not had an increase for the last 7-8 years for our employees’ health
care. Mr. Vowell further discussed another benefit to our employees was the
market increases to employee compensation. He stated that we have been able to
be competitive within the market.
Mr. Vowell went on to discuss the Property Tax rates. He spoke about FY 15, 16,
17 and then in FY20 we were able to keep that flat.
Mr. Vowell gave a property tax breakdown on the dollar. The general ones that
everyone pays for is School District, Fire District and NPC. He stated that the
General Fund is about 8%.
Mr. Vowell discussed the PSPRS Pension Refinancing and stated that in October
of 2022 we decided to refinance our PSPRS. He stated that our debt service
payment is about $1.2 million dollars. He spoke about how this has helped us and
stated that we will stay our cost and budget certainty at $1.2 million dollars without
the worry of it going up.
Bryan Layton spoke about the history of employee compensation. He indicated that
since 2018, we have been able to give anniversary step increases, longevity
adjustments, performance pay, market adjustments and COLA increases. He
spoke about the minimum wage increase and provided some examples, such as a
Deputy Sheriff Cadet who was hired in January 2021 making $42,742 and three
years later a Deputy Sheriff II making $59,761; a Road Maintenance Equipment
Operator I who made $14.81 an hour in December of 2021 is currently making
$20.33 an hour as an Equipment Operator II; and an Intern in Public Works making
$10.50 an hour hired in the summer of 2018, went full time in May 2019 making
$40,438 and is currently a Division Manager making $76,502.
Jayson Vowell spoke about the anticipated challenges. He spoke about the housing
                                                                    15 of 19 
DRAFT

<!-- page 19 -->
of inmates. He stated that we were at 50 inmates and now we are at 30. He stated
that the Sheriff’s office depends on that money. He indicated that we see those
inmate numbers turn down due to Hopi building a new jail. He indicated that the
contract could go away. Mr. Vowell stated that jail medical services are right
around $2 million dollars and will continue to be an expense. Mr. Vowell indicated
that of course inflation will continue to go up and that we have seen a huge jump in
both State Shared Sales Tax and County sales Tax.
Mr. Vowell presented available budget tools. He stated that should sales tax
revenues start declining or if there are significant state cost shifts that we can’t
adapt to that we do have several tools or levers at our disposal to help us absorb
new costs or provide relief until revenues improve. He spoke about the reduction of
county cost of county contribution to healthcare with no impact to service. He
stated that we can freeze or slow capital replacements and vacancies. He further
stated that we can freeze or slow one-time projects and payments. He stated that
we could also spend down some financial reserves.
Mr. Layton discussed the existing and proposed fiscal year 24/25 budget priorities:
those being broadband, the Taylor Road Yard, the Holbrook Complex, the
Heber remodel, and the south county expansion. He further spoke of the big budget
priorities being on the radar is employee compensation, exploring opportunities for
GIS, and other Board of Supervisors priorities that we would like to hear more
about from the Board of Supervisors.  
Mr. Layton discussed the options for employee compensation, indicating that
COLA, performance pay, and one-time salary increases are all options. Supervisor
Peshlakai asked about employees that are capped out at their salary range and
how these options affect those employees.   
Mr. Layton indicated that they should not be capped, that everyone gets COLA
because it takes all the ranges and salaries and moves it up.  He stated that with
performance pay you may not get an increase, but a onetime pay, depending
where you are in the steps.
Supervisor Peshlakai spoke about a specific employee who had dual
responsibilities, did receive two increases because of that, but was told that they
weren’t eligible to receive the additional COLA increase because of the previous
increases.  He stated that they were offered employment elsewhere with additional
incentives, which they took, but that they would have stayed because of the work
environment with the Department and that the Department wanted to keep the
employee because of their productivity.   
Mr. Layton stated that this should have come to Administrations attention to be
reevaluated.
Supervisor Seymore stated that he would like to see more benefits as part of
compensation, like maybe offering three or four more vacation days, that doesn’t
cost anymore out of pocket, but that could be built in as a park to be used for
vacation, grieving days, or maternity leave.
Supervisor Whitesinger inquired if a daycare facility could fall into the same
scenario, indicating if there was a group of parents or caregivers that wished to
forego performance pay or something to that effect.  She stated that it would
support the group of employees looking at childcare.    
Mr. Layton stated that it is very important and that they have looked into a daycare
facility but that the cost to establish a center would be approximately 2.5 to 3 million
dollars.  He indicated that there’s a workgroup at NACo that is trying to address that
in different ways, such as encouraging smaller units like at home daycares. He
stated that he will reach out to that organization and look into it further.
                                                                    16 of 19 
DRAFT

<!-- page 20 -->
Mr. Layton stated that GIS is basic map geography that you can layer and that it
could be used for just about anything.  He stated that it’s a really great tool to assist
in planning, deploying resources and providing information to the public.  He talked
about the high-level maps and indicating that most organizations share this for their
zoning and that it’s much more useful for developers, miners, and homeowners.
Bryan spoke of the desired zoning map vs how we currently use GIS, indicating
that we don’t have enough control points so the maps are not detailed and can be
confusing.  He stated that they would like to bring in a consultant company who
would come in and establish control points that are in more detail than we currently
have and that it would take a couple of months to do. He indicated that they would
like to do an RFQ to see what companies are available in the area and that they
are estimating four hundred to five hundred thousand dollars. He stated that this is
the first step to create a basis of network control points that we can work off of and
then the sky is the limit. He indicated that we are not providing wrong information
now, that it’s just not detailed and that with this they could provide excellent
detailed information on the website, which will help save on staff time due to calls
regarding the current information.  He stated that this is a tool that we need to start
using but that we are not set up to use it yet.   
Mr. Layton talked about a recommended approach that he would like to look at, due
to slowing growth in revenues, potential loss of revenues and potential for State
cost shifts, indicating that it severely restricts the budget capacity to add new
positions.  He stated that if a department has received grants that will displace
some general fund dollars and keep it flat, they would work with them to be creative
and find a solution.   He stated that with the capacity they do have, they would
recommend prioritizing employee compensation.  He indicated that there is fund
capacity which would allow for some one-time projects and if need be, they have
some budget leverage to slow expenses.
Chairman Whiting thanked Mr. Layton for the data regarding what is happening
with the revenues and for the recap on how we are at where we are at this time. 
He recognized the Department Directors and Elected Officials that are involved in
the budget conservations, indicating that how those are done have changed over
the years to try and find the best way to productively engage and find solutions for
what works best.  He indicated that the current process that is in place seems to
work well.  He stated that it has been his experience that during the budget
meetings both sides try to credibly find solutions. He stated that they come in
knowing what the realities are but that both sides work to see what came be done
to accomplish what the departments are asking.  He indicated that it has already
been vetted through and both sides try to find a way forward, which he feels is
healthier.  He talked about previous years when they would come in and make a
pitch and the Board would have to decide who gets what, which created winners
and losers and that it was not a good relationship with the Departments and the
Board of Supervisors.  He stated that the way it’s done now, the intended outcome
is to find a way to have a balanced budget and to meaningfully partner with each of
the Department Directors and Elected Officials on ways forward.
Mr. Layton stated that they try to make things work and that communication with
the Department Directors and Elected Officials has been key, before coming before
the Board.
Supervisor Whiting stated that he trusts our team, that their interests are heard
and that they want to be able to reward or compensate the employees who are
going above and beyond. He stated that Brad Carlyon does a great job of talking
about some of the responsibilities that he has and the pressure he feels to take
                                                                    17 of 19 
DRAFT

<!-- page 21 -->
care of the obligations and make sure that those needs are met.  He stated that it
seems like it is working better now, that they are trying to partner and find solution,
whereas the other process didn’t allow for some of those conversations to occur. 
He indicated that he wants to support them, that they have to be balanced, that the
conversation has to be credible and that they have to look at it as a whole due to
the responsibility for the services that have to be provided.  He stated that the
feedback that they get from the upcoming budget meetings is important and
appreciated.  He indicated that he hopes it has moved to a place where there is
trust and where they can try and find the best pathway forward to give the
departments what they need and what they have been asked for by citizens, but
that they have to do that within the budget responsibilities.     
Supervisor Whitesinger stated that this is a good approach, that each department
knows the needs within their departments.  She further stated that for the Board to
make the assumption that they know what is needed in each department is unreal
and that having those conversations is important. She indicated that having a
variety of capacity that could potentially meet our needs is important when looking
at long term.   She talked about when looking at potential budget shortfalls years
down the road, and the departments are aware of that, having these conversations
and changing the relationships is really important and has seemed to become more
positive. She stated that the past year budget cycle was a positive conversation in
trying to best understand the organization and the department’s needs.  She
shared her appreciation for being able to be thoughtful, rather than being
restrictive. 
Mr. Layton stated that the Department Directors and Elected Officials have been
fantastic and indicated that we wouldn’t be where we are financially right now
without their cooperation and diligence.  He further stated that they try to be
creative in finding ways to make things happen.
Supervisor Peshlakai stated that the Detention Center in Hopi should be in
operation by next month, that he has been part of some of their internal meetings,
and that they are planning on doing their opening in March 2024.  He stated that
with the presentation his only question was regarding the compensation for
employees, and that he didn’t know until after the fact the situation with the
employee that he spoke of previously. 
Supervisor Seymore stated that the greatest resources are the employees and
the assets that are in the buildings.  He stated that we need to build a bigger, better
stronger, and that things will be better.  He further stated that we need to always
have a vision, to make sure that we know what that vision is and that we continue
to look at that vision.  He indicated that we had the opportunity to make a difference
with one-time funds and that we are hoping that the broadband is something that
goes forward since we won’t have those funds again, that we don’t have access but
that we have surplus.  He stated that we shouldn’t put ourselves in the corner, that
they shouldn’t be opposed to raising a tax, if it maintains the standard.  He
indicated that there are sometimes costs that are associated with standards for
safety, protection and services that are provided.  He stated that they need to stay
abreast of the employees because everybody’s competing for good sources of
employees.  He indicated that he will advocate for the compensation package to
always compensate those for the services that they do. 
Mr. Layton stated that these are the kind of parameters that they hoped for in this
discussion and that they will work to find new solutions.  He indicated that with the
Boards support these are the parameters that they can work with. 
Supervisor Whiting stated that he liked the comments regarding being creative to
                                                                    18 of 19

find solutions that meet the needs of the employees.  He stated that you see it a lot
in the workplace, that pay matters, but that ultimately culture matters and that

that the team is always innovating and looking, and he encouraged that they look at

Board of Supervisors Regular 2. c.        
Meeting Date: 03/12/2024  
Title: ARRF Funds request
Submitted By: Maxine Tyler, Executive Assistant
Department: County Attorney
Motion before the Board:
Alice's Place is requesting $600.00 in Anti-Racketeering Revolving Funds to assist in their 2024 Murder
Mystery Dinner fundraiser
Background:
Alice's Place offers Domestic Violence & Sexual Violence Services.  They are able to provide a safe and
respectful place to support victims through shelter, advocacy, education and many resources.  Examples
of their services are 24 hour crisis hotline, emergency shelter, transportation, safety planning, and more. 
Attachments
Request for ARRF Funds 
Form Review
Inbox Reviewed By Date
County Attorney Brad Carlyon 02/21/2024 09:08 AM
Form Started By: Maxine Tyler Started On: 02/21/2024 08:55 AM
Final Approval Date: 02/21/2024

<!-- page 456 -->
Board of Supervisors Regular 2. d.        
Meeting Date: 03/12/2024  
Title: Reissuance of Stale Dated Checks
Submitted For: Kari Lopez, Treasurer  Submitted By: Olivia Todd, Chief Deputy Treasurer
Department: Treasurer
Motion before the Board:
Reissuance of Stale Dated Checks
Background:
Treasurer's checks were issued as refunds for overpaid property taxes or for investor payments. The
checks were voided because they had been outstanding for a year or more. The payees were contacted
and are requesting replacement checks. All have completed the required Affidavit For Issuance of
Duplicate Warrant form. Upon approval, a new check will be issued to the individual(s) pursuant to
A.R.S. §11-632.

Title: Back Tax Land Sale

Motion before the Board:
Approval of 19 Back Tax Land Parcels sold over the counter in the amount of $10,001.14
Background:

Title: Microsoft Annual Contract
Submitted By: Leah Thomas, Deputy Clerk of the Board of Supervisors
Department: Information Technology
Motion before the Board:
Second Year Microsoft Agreement
Background:
Navajo County has been running Microsoft products for many years and has made a large investment of
time and training to provide a high level of support for the employees.  This is the agreement for the
second year of the contract that is for software that flows through the entire County from local PC
operating systems, Excel, and Word to the Servers that store all the data for the different Departments and
Offices.  This also includes opportunities to leverage Cloud services including email and storage in
Microsoft's secure government cloud.  This agreement shows that there are no additional products being
added to the current three year contract.

Board of Supervisors Regular 3. a. 1.        
Meeting Date: 03/12/2024  
Title: Navajo County Library District Annual Book Sale Proposal
Submitted For: Jennifer Volkert, Library District Director 
Submitted By: Kathleen Pearson, Programs Coordinator
Department: NC Library District
Presented By: Katy Pearson and Alicia Kumar
Motion before the Board:
Consideration of Navajo County Library District holding an annual book sale for books discarded by the
district and its member libraries.
Background:
Most of the member libraries have book sales annually, but still have a large amount of books to dispose
of after. There are no book recycling services in north-eastern Arizona and the thrift stores and used
bookstores do not have the capacity to accept donations as large as the one the library district would need
to make annually. In spite of other efforts to dispose of these books, without throwing them away, we still
have a large quantity left every year. 
Recommendation: 
Navajo County Library District recommends holding an annual book sale to dispose of these books and
still benefit library users in Navajo County and their guests. The book sale proceeds would go directly
back into purchasing more library materials  and services for the district.

Board of Supervisors Regular 5. a.        
Meeting Date: 03/12/2024  
Title: Memorandum of Agreement with White Mountain Apache Tribe
Submitted For: Madhav Mundle, Public Works Director 
Submitted By: Alexandria Porras, Administrative Specialist
Department: Public Works
Presented By: Madhav Mundle
Motion before the Board:
Consideration of approving the Memorandum of Agreement (MOA) between the White Mountain
Apache Tribe Division of Transportation and Navajo County for assistance in road projects.
Background:
The White Mountain Apache Tribe has worked with the Bureau of Indian Affairs Western Region to
maintain roads within the reservation for many years. In recent years, the Tribe has built up their own
Division of Transportation to assist in keeping the maintenance of routes in the reservation and have
partnered with the County through a previous MOA which has expired. This MOA allows the County to
assist the White Mountain Apache Tribe Division of Transportation (WMAT DOT) by acting as a
partner in supplying or transporting materials for projects; providing project planning assistance and
assisting in road repair or maintenance activities if agreed upon by the Board of Supervisors. Additional
projects may be written into an Agreement and added to this MOA by the authorized representatives of
participating parties and shall be governed by the terms of this MOA.
Recommendation: 
Staff recommends approval.

<!-- page 473 -->
MEMORANDUM OF AGREEMENT BETWEEN 
WHITE MOUNTAIN APACHE TRIBE DIVISION OF TRANSPORTATION 
AND 
NAVAJO COUNTY 
 
 
THIS MEMORANDUM OF AGREEMENT ("MOA'') is entered into and between Navajo 
County, a political subdivision of the State Of Arizona (the "County"), and the White 
Mountain Apache Tribe. 
 
Article I 
Authority 
 
1.1 The county is authorized by A.R.S. 11-251 and 11- 952 to enter into intergovernmental 
agreements for cooperative action with agencies of the United States and/or Tribal 
governments. 
 
1.2 A.R.S. 11-251 (4) authorizes the county to maintain all public roads within its 
boundaries. 
 
1.3 A.R.S. 11-251 (29) authorizes the Board of Supervisors of the County to enter into 
agreements for the maintenance of highways, including those which pass through 
Indian lands, with the government of the United States, acting through its duly 
authorized officers or agents pursuant to any act of Congress. 
 
1.4 The above statutes have been interpreted by the Arizona Attorney General in Opinion 
No. 186-029 to authorize the expenditure of funds for the maintenance of all public 
roads within Indian country (as defined in 18 U.S.C. 1151). 
 
1.5 The code of Federal Regulations provides in 25 C.F.R. 170.6 that "The administration 
and maintenance of Indian Reservation Roads and Bridges is basically a function of 
the local Government Subject to availability of funds, the Commissioner (of Indian 
Affairs) shall maintain, or cause to be maintained, those approved roads on the 
Federal-Aid Indian Road System." 
 
1.6 25 C.F.R. 170.7 provides that Tribes may enter into an agreement with the State 
(which is defined to include political subdivisions such as county) for cooperation in 
the maintenance of certain Indian Reservation Roads and Bridges, especially at those 
locations where road projects serve on non-Indian land as well as Indian land. 
 
1.7 The White Mountain Apache Tribe may retain all funding received pursuant to 
this MOA without depositing the same in the Miscellaneous Receipts Account of 
the United States Treasury. The White Mountain Apache Tribe has the authority to 
retain these funds and use them for the road maintenance that supplements but does 
not replace maintenance projects funded by the appropriate funds. Pursuant to the 
broad authorities of the Secretary of Interior over Indian Affairs, the regulations

<!-- page 474 -->
in 25 C.F.R. Part 170, and the broad authorities in Title 23 U.S.C.A. (Federal 
Lands Highways Programs) governing Indian Reservation roads and 
intergovernmental cooperation, the White Mountain Apache Tribe, State/County 
Cooperation on road maintenance of Indian reservation roads is permitted. 
 
Article II 
Purpose 
 
2.1 Navajo County has agreed to provide funds to the White Mountain Apache Tribe for 
the White Mountain Apache Tribe's road-related activities on the portion of the White 
Mountain Apache Indian Reservation within the boundaries of Navajo County, 
Arizona. The White Mountain Apache Tribe affirms that it has the ability to perform 
such road-related activities and agrees to accept the funds granted by the County 
pursuant to this MOA. 
 
2.2 The parties intend for this MOA to serve as the master agreement between them for 
road-related maintenance activities and the funding thereof, each such activity to be 
described in a Task agreement to the attached hereto. Task Agreements shall describe 
the effective date, term, amount of Allocated Funds, funding source(s), purpose(s), 
disbursement schedule and requirements, accounting requirements, and such other 
terms and conditions as the parties may mutually agree upon, and shall be attached 
hereto upon approval by both parties. Each Task Agreement shall be subject to the 
terms and conditions of the MOA. 
 
Article III 
Responsibilities 
 
3.1 The Navajo County Board of Supervisors, by motion duly passed at the Board's regular 
meeting on the date shown in each Task Agreement, shall have approved the expenditure 
of specific funds (the "Allocated Funds") for specified road-related activities within that 
portion of the White Mountain Apache Reservation located within one or more 
Supervisorial Districts (this refers to the five districts into which the County is divided 
pursuant to the Arizona Revised Statues). 
 
3.2 The Allocated Funds shall be used by the White Mountain Apache Tribe solely for the 
purpose(s) specified in each Task Agreement. 
 
3.3 The Allocated Funds are intended to supplement other monies that are or may become 
available to the White Mountain Apache Tribe for road-related activities within that 
portion of the White Mountain Apache Reservation located within Supervisorial 
District(s) indicated in each Task Agreement. The receipt of the Allocated Funds shall 
not cause other monies to be diverted or allocated to projects outside that portion of the 
White Mountain Apache Reservation within Supervisorial District(s) indicated in each 
Task Agreement. 
 
3.4 The Allocated Funds shall be disbursed to the White Mountain Apache Tribe as shown in

<!-- page 475 -->
each Task Agreement and shall be used solely for the purpose(s) specified therein. The 
procedure for disbursing the Allocated Funds and accounting for their expenditure shall 
be mutually agreed upon by the respective financial officers of the parties and shall be 
indicated in each Task Agreement. 
 
3.5 Any Allocated Funds that are unused by the date(s) or time frame(s) shown in each Task 
Agreement shall be refunded to the county within 30 days unless the Task Agreement 
has been extended or renewed by mutual written consent of the county and White 
Mountain Apache Tribe. Upon the mutual written consent of the county and White 
Mountain Apache Tribe, this MOA and/or each Task Agreement may be renewed for 
one or more additional periods. 
 
3.6 The funding described in each Task Agreement is subject to the availability of funds to 
the County from the source(s) indicated in the Task Agreement. 
 
3.7 Each Party warrants that this MOA has been executed by the duly authorized official 
and that all requisite action has been taken for this MOA to be valid and binding 
obligation of the part y. 
 
Article IV 
Key Officials 
 
4.1 The key officials for each party are as follows: 
 
Public Works Director Transportation Director 
Navajo County WMAT – Division of Transportation 
            100 W Public Works Dr P.O. Box 1710 
Holbrook. AZ 86025 Whiteriver, AZ  85941 
(928) 524-4000 (928) 338-5155 
 
Article V 
Reporting, Billing and Payment 
 
5.1 Records and Reports: Records and reports will be maintained by both the County and the 
White Mountain Apache Tribe for all activities, funding and expenditures related to this 
MOA and each Task Agreement. Upon request by either party, the other party shall 
provide access to or copies of all such records and report s. 
 
5.2 Funding: Disbursement of Allocated Funds to the White Mountain Apache Tribe and 
accounting for expenditures by the White Mountain Apache Tribe shall be made in 
accordance with each Task Agreement. 
 
Article VI 
Term of MOA 
 
6.1 This MOA becomes effective on the date of the last signature and shall remain in force

<!-- page 476 -->
and effect for 5 years. Either party may propose amendments to this MOA or any 
Task Agreement by submitting them in writing to the other party at least 30 days 
before the proposed implementation date. Amendments must be approved in writing 
by both parties. 
 
6.2 In the event of any controversy which may arise out of this MOA or any Task 
Agreement, the County and White Mountain Apache Tribe agree to select a 
procedure and forum mutually acceptable to them to informally resolve such dispute. 
 
6.3 Appropriation (Anti Deficiency Act. 31 U.S.C. 1341): Nothing contained in this 
Agreement shall be construed as binding the White Mountain Apache Tribe to expend 
in any one fiscal year any sum in excess of appropriations made by Congress, as 
augmented by finding pursuant to this MOA, for the purpose of this Agreement for 
that fiscal year, or other obligation for the further expenditure of money in excess of 
such appropriations. 
 
6.4 Nothing in this MOA is intended to, nor shall it be interpreted to grant or alter any 
right-of-way easement over roads on the White Mountain Apache Reservation or grant 
or alter the existing jurisdiction of any government over Indian reservation roads on the 
White Mountain Apache Reservation. 
 
6.5 Nothing in this MOA is intended to, nor shall it be interpreted to allocate or shift any 
current or future liability of a party to this MOA due to such party's acts or omissions 
to the other party to this MOA. 
 
6.6 Nothing in this MOA is intended to, nor shall it be interpreted to establish or enhance 
any rights by any person, government, or other entity not a party to this MOA. Nothing 
in this MOA shall prevent the White Mountain Apache Tribe from consulting with 
representatives of Navajo County, Arizona, concerning the White Mountain Apache 
Tribe's road maintenance activities pursuant to this MOA. 
 
Article VII 
Authorized Signatures 
 
NAVAJO COUNTY: 
 
 
 
     
By: Chairman, Board of Supervisors  Date 
 
 
 
     
By: Deputy County Attorney  Date

<!-- page 477 -->
WHITE MOUNTAIN APACHE TRIBE: 
 
 
 
     
By: Chairman  Date

Board of Supervisors Regular 5. b.        
Meeting Date: 03/12/2024  
Title: Intergovernmental Agreement for Joseph City Sidewalk Improvements - Construction
Submitted For: William Flake, Assistant County Engineer 
Submitted By: Alexandria Porras, Administrative Specialist
Department: Public Works
Presented By: Madhav Mundle
Motion before the Board:
Consideration of an Intergovernmental Agreement between the State of Arizona and Navajo County for
federal Transportation Alternatives Grant for a sidewalk project in the Joseph City area.
Background:
Navajo County is the recipient of a federal Transportation Alternatives (TA) Grant. The TA grant is part
of a program that provides federal funding for a variety of smaller-scale transportation projects and
activities, including pedestrian-type projects.  The improvements proposed under this Agreement consist
of Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA) compliant sidewalks, multi-use pathways and crossings in
Joseph City. The project provides pedestrian connectivity between the Elementary School and the
Junior/Senior High School. The County has the majority of the design completed by an engineering
consultant, and the State (ADOT) will advertise, bid and award, and administer the construction of the
project.
 
Recommendation: 
Staff recommends approval.

gency will administer the design, and the State will advertise, bid and award, and

5. The foregoing Recitals and Exhibit A shall be incorporated into this Agreement. 
In consideration of the mutual terms expressed herein, the Parties agree as follows: 
 
 
 
II. SCOPE OF WORK 
 
1. The Parties agree:  
 
a. The Project will be completed, accepted, and paid for in accordance with the 
requirements of the Project plans and specifications.  
 
b. The final cost estimate may exceed the initial estimate identified in Exhibit A, and in 
such case, the Local Agency is responsible for and agrees to pay, the difference prior to 
bid advertisement. 
 
c. The final Project amount may exceed the initial estimate(s) identified in Exhibit A, and 
in such case, the Local Agency is responsible for, and agrees to pay, any and all actual 
costs exceeding the initial estimate. If the final Project amount is less than the initial 
estimate, the difference between the final bid amount and the initial estimate will be de-
obligated or otherwise released from the Project and returned to the State. The Local 
Agency acknowledges it remains responsible for actual costs and agrees to pay 
according to the terms of this Agreement.  
 
d. The Local Agency and ADOT will each separately file a Notice of Intent (NOI) under the 
Construction General Permit (CGP) with the Arizona Department of Environmental 
Quality (ADEQ) before construction begins, if applicable to the Project. 
 
 2. The State will: 
 
a. Execute this Agreement, and if the Project is approved by FHWA and funds for the 
Project are available, be the Local Agency’s designated agent for the Project. 
 
b. After this Agreement is executed, and prior to performing or authorizing any work on 
the Project, invoice the Local Agency for the Local Agency’s share of the initial Project 
Development Administration (PDA) costs, estimated at $1,710. If PDA costs exceed the 
estimate during the review of design, notify the Local Agency, obtain concurrence prior 
to continuing with the review of design, and invoice as determined by ADOT and the 
Local Agency for additional costs to complete PDA for the Project. After the Project costs 
are finalized invoice or reimburse the Local Agency for the difference between actual 
costs and the amount the Local Agency has paid for PDA. 
 
c. After receipt of the PDA costs, review design plans, specifications, cost estimates and 
other such documents required for the construction bidding and construction of the 
Project, including scoping/design plans and documents required by FHWA to qualify 
projects for and to receive federal funds; provide design review comments to the Local 
Agency  as appropriate.

d. After completion of design review and prior to bid advertisement, invoice the Local

Project construction costs, estimated at $48,835. After the Project costs for construction 
are finalized, the State will either invoice or reimburse the Local Agency for the 
difference between estimated and actual costs. De-obligate or otherwise release any 
remaining federal funds from the design phase of the Project. 
 
e. After receipt of the actual PDA costs, if applicable, and the Local Agency’s estimated

to FHWA with the recommendation that the maximum federal funds programmed for

f. After receipt of FHWA authorization, proceed to advertise for, receive and open bids,

i. Not be obligated to maintain the Project, should the Local Agency fail to budget or 
provide for proper and perpetual maintenance as set forth in this Agreement.

Way Procedures Manual (23 CFR 710.201). Additionally the Local Agency shall certify 
that all real estate related activities requiring licensure are performed by licensed 
individuals as defined by the Arizona Department of Real Estate (A.R.S. §§ 32-2121 & 
32-2122).  
 
i. Not permit or allow any encroachments on or private use of the right of way, except 
those authorized by permit. In the event of any unauthorized encroachment or 
improper use, the Local Agency shall take all necessary steps to remove or prevent any 
such encroachment or use. Provide a copy of encroachment permits issued within the 
Project limits to the State.  
 
j. Automatically grant to the State, by execution of this Agreement, its agents and/or 
contractors, without cost, the temporary right to enter the Local Agency’s rights of way, 
as required, to conduct any and all construction and preconstruction related activities

<!-- page 483 -->
IGA 24-0009597-I 
 
 
Page 5 of 11 
 
for the Project, on, to and over said Local Agency’s rights of way. This temporary right 
will expire with completion of the Project. 
k. Investigate and document utilities within the Project limits; submit findings to ADOT 
determining prior rights or no prior rights; approve a location within the final right of 
way to re-establish the prior rights location for those utilities with prior rights. 
 
l. Be obligated to incur any expenditure should unforeseen conditions or circumstances 
increase Project costs. Be responsible for the cost of any Local Agency requested 
changes to the scope of work of the Project, such changes will require State and FHWA 
approval. Be responsible for any contractor claims for additional compensation caused 
by Project delay attributable to the Local Agency. Payment for these costs will be made 
to the State within 30 days of receipt of an invoice from the State. 
 
m. After notification of final acceptance by the State, assume and maintain full 
responsibility of the Project, including Storm Water Pollution Prevention Plans (SWPPP) 
inspections, maintenance, and required documentation, until final stabilization is 
reached. Provide the NOI number to the State and the Contractor, accept CGP 
responsibilities at time of transfer, and file a NOT with ADEQ when final stabilization is 
reached, as applicable. 
 
n. After completion and final acceptance of the Project, agree to maintain and assume full 
responsibility of the Project and all Project components. 
 
 
III. MISCELLANEOUS PROVISIONS 
 
1. Effective Date. This Agreement shall become effective upon signing and dating of all Parties. 
 
2. Amendments. Any change or modification to the Project will only occur with the mutual 
written consent of both Parties. 
 
3. Duration. The terms, conditions and provisions of this Agreement shall remain in full force 
and effect until completion of the Project and all related deposits and/or reimbursements 
are made. Any and all obligations of maintenance hereunder shall remain perpetual and 
shall survive any termination hereof and the assignment or assumption of this Agreement 
or the Project by another competent jurisdiction or entity. 
 
4. Cancellation. This Agreement may be cancelled at any time up to 30 days before the award 
of the Project contract, so long as the cancelling Party provides at least 30 days’ prior 
written notice to the other Party. It is understood and agreed that, in the event the Local 
Agency terminates this Agreement, the Local Agency shall be responsible for all costs 
incurred by the State up to the time of termination. It is further understood and agreed that 
in the event the Local Agency terminates this Agreement, the State shall in no way be 
obligated to complete or maintain the Project.   
 
5. Indemnification. The Local Agency shall indemnify, defend, and hold harmless the State, any 
of its departments, agencies, boards, commissions, officers or employees (collectively 
referred to in this paragraph as the “State”) from any and all claims, demands, suits, actions, 
proceedings, loss, cost and damages of every kind and description, including reasonable 
attorneys' fees and/or litigation expenses (collectively referred to in this paragraph as the

<!-- page 484 -->
IGA 24-0009597-I 
 
 
Page 6 of 11 
 
“Claims”), which may be brought or made against or incurred by the State on account of loss 
of or damage to any property or for injuries to or death of any person, to the extent caused 
by, arising out of, or contributed to, by reasons of any alleged act, omission, professional 
error, fault, mistake, or negligence of the Local Agency, its employees, officers, directors, 
agents, representatives, or contractors, their employees, agents, or representatives in 
connection with or incident to the performance of this Agreement. The Local Agency’s 
obligations under this paragraph shall not extend to any Claims to the extent caused by the 
negligence of the State, except the obligation does apply to any negligence of the Local 
Agency which may be legally imputed to the State by virtue of the State’s ownership or 
possession of land. The Local Agency’s obligations under this paragraph shall survive the 
termination of this Agreement. 
 
6. Third-Party Indemnification. The State shall include Section 107.13 of the 2021 version of 
the Arizona Department of Transportation Standard Specifications for Road and Bridge 
Construction, incorporated into this Agreement by reference, in the State’s contract with 
any and all contractors, of which the Local Agency shall be specifically named as a third-
party beneficiary. This provision may not be amended without the approval of the Local 
Agency.  
 
7. Programmed Federal Funds. The cost of design review, construction and construction 
engineering work under this Agreement is to be covered by the federal funds programmed 
for this Project, up to the maximum available. The Local Agency acknowledges that actual 
Project costs may exceed the maximum available amount of federal funds, or that certain 
costs may not be accepted by FHWA as eligible for federal funds. Therefore, the Local 
Agency agrees to pay the difference between actual costs of the Project and the federal 
funds received.  
 
8. Termination of Federal Funding. Should the federal funding related to this Project be 
terminated or reduced by the federal government, or Congress rescinds, fails to renew, or 
otherwise reduces apportionments or obligation authority, the State shall in no way be 
obligated for funding or liable for any past, current or future expenses under this 
Agreement. 
 
9. Indirect Costs. The cost of the Project under this Agreement includes indirect costs 
approved by FHWA, as applicable. 
 
10. Federal Funding Accountability and Transparency Act. The Parties warrant compliance with 
the Federal Funding Accountability and Transparency Act of 2006 and associated 2008 
Amendments (the “Act”). Additionally, in a timely manner, the Local Agency will provide 
information that is requested by the State to enable the State to comply with the 
requirements of the Act, as may be applicable. 
 
11. Governing Law. This Agreement shall be governed by and construed in accordance with 
Arizona laws. 
 
12. Conflicts of Interest. This Agreement may be cancelled in accordance with A.R.S. § 38-511. 
 
13. Inspection and Audit. The Local Agency shall retain all books, accounts, reports, files and 
other records relating to this Agreement which shall be subject at all reasonable times to 
inspection and audit by the State for five years after completion of the Project. Such records

<!-- page 485 -->
IGA 24-0009597-I 
 
 
Page 7 of 11 
 
shall be produced by the Local Agency, electronically or at the State office as set forth in this 
Agreement, at the request of ADOT. 
 
14. Title VI. The Local Agency acknowledges and will comply with Title VI of the Civil Rights Act 
Of 1964. 
 
15. Non-Discrimination. This Agreement is subject to all applicable provisions of the Americans 
with Disabilities Act (Public Law 101-336, 42 U.S.C. 12101-12213) and all applicable federal 
regulations under the Act, including 28 CFR Parts 35 and 36. The Parties to this Agreement 
shall comply with Executive Order Number 2009-09, as amended by Executive Order 2023-
01, issued by the Governor of the State of Arizona and incorporated in this Agreement by 
reference regarding “Non-Discrimination.” 
 
16. Non-Availability of Funds. Every obligation of the State under this Agreement is conditioned 
upon the availability of funds appropriated or allocated for the fulfillment of such 
obligations. If funds are not allocated and available for the continuance of this Agreement, 
this Agreement may be terminated by the State at the end of the period for which the funds 
are available. No liability shall accrue to the State in the event this provision is exercised, 
and the State shall not be obligated or liable for any future payments as a result of 
termination under this paragraph. 
 
17. Arbitration. In the event of any controversy, which may arise out of this Agreement, the 
Parties agree to abide by arbitration as is set forth for public works contracts if required by 
A.R.S. § 12-1518. 
 
18. E-Verify. The Parties shall comply with the applicable requirements of A.R.S. § 41-4401. 
 
19. Contractor Certifications. The Parties shall certify that all contractors comply with the 
applicable requirements of A.R.S. §§ 35-393.01 and 35-394.  
 
20. Other Applicable Laws. The Parties shall comply with all applicable laws, rules, regulations 
and ordinances, as may be amended.
